 .An important facet of the second phase was to attain "stra-

Ivas a "defensive/ offensive " strategy which had the follmring ob -
jectives :

1) to develop strong multi-division forces in dispersed areas
t hat Here secure and accessible to supplies; 2) to entice W
forces into prepared enemy positions so that the entrenched

3) to continue country-,vide guerrilla action to tie dmrn Allied
fo rces , destroy smaLl units ) and extend control.

t he highlands) and then to launch an offensive in one or more
di stricts . The enemy had thus hoped in 1966 to launch ever-l.arger
attacks in the highlands) to concentrate his troops and firepmrer)

 4. Results of the CINCPAC Planning Conference

 . On 12 February, the results of the CINCPAC Conference were
published . 46/

 The concept of operations for 1966 had been more completely
spelled out. The three basic military objectives had by thi s time grown
to four. Now there were two separate obj ect ives,

 1. To extend GVN dominion, direction, and control over
 SVN, and

 2. To defeat the VC and PAVN forces in lLRVN and forc e
 their withdrawal,

 instead of the old task Hhich combined both of these . In achieving the
 objective for extending GVN domination, ' US forces ' tasks were very care-
fully spelled out as "assisting the RVNAF in the conduct of clearing and
securing the civic action operations ... assist and reinforce other US
mis s ion agencies , and assist the RVNAF to defend major political, eco-
nomic , food producing population centers ." The object of defeating the
VC and PAVN forces required more direct act ion such as conducting sus-
tained coordinated offensive operations against the enemy, conducting
air offensives , raids and special operations against enemy "I-lar zones and
bas e areas to render them unusable . In general, "US military operations
are aimed at creating operation environ.ment and opportunity for the GVN
to gain control and establish security of main food producing areas in
order to fe ed the :people, deny food ~o the enemy, bolster the economy,
to cause the enemy to :import or fight for food. " In explaining the US
6~phasis on search and destroy, the m2morandum stated that such opera-
t :Lons "against VC/PAVN forces and base areas attrite VC/PAVN main forces
and destroy VC base areas and in-country supplies . These operations ,
alt hou~h contributory to, are not a part of the r~ral construct:Lon effort ,
per se, but are constituted concomitantly Hith it. I t is clear that a
kno"lffi and ex;pected VC/PAVN build-up, the prime focus of combat capable
units of US/F~1A}' and RVRAlc forces must be directed to the search and
destroy effort ."

 CINCPAC conceded that ;

 This concept of employment of forces is of long standing;
 hOI.rever , the lack of sufficient ARVN regular forces for offen-
 sive operations plus the increasing VC strength have resulted

 in local RVN military commanders utilizing the security forces
 (primarily R}') PF) in offensive actions against hard core VC

 the GVN. 'J'hese deployments allmr RVNAF forces to be employed

 II. PROGP~M No . 3

 A. Interlude

 As far as the actual conduct of ground operations in Vietnam was
 conc erned) the period of time from 1 May 1965 to 1 November 1965 was
 spent in buj.lding up combat and logistical forces and learning to employ
 them effectively . This was followed by a period from 1 November 1965 to
 1 May 1966) in which the deployment of U. S. forces was extended tO,vard
 the frontiers ) logistical support vTas exercised in furnishing support to
 troops in sustained combat ) and commanders were indoctrinated on the
 techniques of sustaj.ned ground combat .

 The NVA/VC avoided initiating actions which might result in
 large and unacceptable casualties from the firepower of Allied
 forc es . During the year the enemy became increasingly cautious
 in the face of increased Allied strength . The enemy tended to
 attack only 'Then he had overvThelming superiority of numbers )
 such as during the attack in March on the Special Forces outpost
 at A Shau . VC tactics ,-Tere designed to conserve main force
 strengths for the most opportune targets. The ~WA/VC avoided
 attacking large Allied units of regiment or brigade size) but
 did attack is olated battalions and companies using sufficient
 strength to insure great numerical superiority . It was typical
 of th~ ener~ to attack with one-third of his available force
 and to employ the remaining tiw-thirds of the units to set up
 an ambush of the Allied relief column . During attacks the
 NVA/VC used a hugging tactic as a means of protecting them-
 selves from Allied artillery and air strikes . The enemy often
 withdrew' by small squad-sized increments) using multiple routes .
 To defend against surveillance and artillery and air strikes)
 the enemy dispersed into the jungle in smail units) moved fre-
 quently) and made maximum use of darkness and periods of Iml
 vis ib ility . . . ~/

 It is interesting to note) however) the pattern formed by VillCV ' s
 operations during 1966. In the I Corps area) the large-scale operations
 conducted by the Marines in the spring of the year were for the most
 part located along the coast of the southern part of the area) in the
 Provinces of Quang Tin and Quang Ngai.

 Beginning vr.ith Operation DOlJ13LE EAGLE I ( 28 January to 17 February );
 they progressed through DOUBLE EAGLE II (19 February to 1 March);
 Operation UTAH (4 March ); Operation TEXAS (18 March ); and Operation
 HOT SPRIl'TGS on 21 April. All of these operations \-Tere keyed on intelli-
 gence of an enemy build-up i n and around Quang Ngai . Contact on these
 operatj.ons ranged from sporadic to contact with a NVA regiment on Opera-
 tion UTAH. '1'he major exception to the location of operations in this area
 "las Operation OREGON vlhich "lvas conducted in the vicinity of Thua Thien in
 late March .

 Operations in the II Corps Tactical Zone in 1966 displayed a Sllll -
 l ar pattern . The two key areas of concern in II Corps Here the coastal
 plains in Binh Dinh Province and near Tuy Hoa) and the Central Highland
 Plateau area arotmd Pleiku . Although General Westmoreland appeared to
 be, impatient to find the enemy 8nd defeat him in the relatively sparsely
 populated plateau area) most of the operations in the first half of the
 year which resulted in significant contact I"ith the enemy took place
 near the Coastal Plains . The first operation of the year) which ran
 from 28 January to 4 February) was Operation MASHER) renamed Operation
 WHITE lUNG because of the concern over public reaction to the image
 portrayed by the name ItMASHER. It

 Operation WHITE WING continued until 6 March . This operation in
 the Bong Son and An Lao Valley region made heavy contact with 1 VC and
 1 NVA regiment. ItlV'as follovTed by DAVEY CROCKETT ( 4-16 May ) and
 CRAZY HORSE (17 May to 5 June ) ) both in the same area .

 other significant operations in the spring of the year ,,,ere Opera -
 tions VAN BUREN and HARRISON which) together) ran from 19 January through

 1st Brigade of the 101st Aj.rborne Division) vTere designed to protect the
 rice harvest in that area .

 Operations in the III Corps area began with Operation MARAUDER in
 Hau Nghia and Long An Provinces on 7 January; Operation CRIMP) along the
 Hau Nghia/ Binh Duong border; and Operation BUCKSKIN near ChuChi on
 11 January .

 In February) Operation MASTIFF into the Michelin Plantation) and
 Operation .MJl.LLET in Phouc Tuy Province ) were carried out . Neither
 Operation produced substantial enemy kills) but hopefully they were
 instrQmental in breaking up VC supply and cowmand and control facilities .
 By 10 February) hOlV'ever) Operation ROLLING STONE had been kicked off and
 by 20 September it had encountered a l)OOO-man VC force in Binh Duong.
 On 7 March) another search and destroy operation in Binh Duong) Operation
 SILVER CITY,triggered a four-hour attack by the enemy against 173rd Air-
 borne Brigade) one of the participating units. On 24 April) the center
 of operat ions moved further north when BIRlVIINGHAM began a thrust into
 Tay Ninh . The most significant part of BlRi'tlINGHAl.VI '\vas the capture of
 vast quantities of enemy supplies and facilities despite the small number
 of enemy killed. By May of 1966) the 1st Cav8.1ry Division was operating
 in the Central Highlands) the 1st Infantry Division ';.ras in operation
 north of Saigon) vrhile the 25th Infantry Division had one brigade operat -
 ing with the 1st Cavalry Division on the Central Plateau) with the other

battalions scheduled to be in Vietnam by the end of 1966 to 79 and the

 CINCPAC ' s requirements were based on a new concept for Vietnam .
 The four basic objectives remained as they had been set forth in CINCPAC ' s
 February concept . A ne"T i.tem in the June concept yTaS that US/ FWMAF and
 RVNAF general reserves and ARYrJ corps reserve forces would conduct sus-

 During this time, tyTO slightly different estimates of enemy

 i ng up to 15 battalion equivalents (9,000 personnel ) per month into South

 enemy could train 7 VC battalion equivalents (3,500 personnel ) per month

 i ntentions was that he "rould attempt to reinforce at the rate of 18 . 5
 battalion equivalents ( 11. 5 NVA, and 7 VC ) per month, which "rou~d give
 him a maximum build-up total of 180 battalion equivalents by March 1967,
 at i.,rhich time losses "TQuld exceed inputs and total VC strength "rould

 The estimate of VC strength given in NIE 14 . 3- 66, 2/ was as follows :
 The total Communist force in South Vietnam was estimated to be beJnrei:m
 260, 000 and 280, 000 . The major combat elements included some 38, 000
 North Vietnamese troops, approximately 63 , 000 regular main and local

 placements) but present evidence suggested that the probable infiltration

 The requirements for CY 1967 were basically considered to be

 strength of U.S. force s in Vietnam would be 90 maneuver battalions and
 542,588 personnel.

 r educe the total monetary circulation. Retail price indices

 He then l isted five primary threa.ts : The first was wage stability . There

 programming rise to r ate of over 2~ 7 billion piasters was orig-

 total GVN civil and milital'y expenditures vrere about 55 billion piasters

 I n essence, what Ambassador Lodge seemed to be looking for liTaS a
 solution Ivhich would balance the conflicting inexorables , eSIlecially
 those of battle and inflation. He ended up ~y straddling the fence .
 He stated that he believed that we should "bring as massive an American
 military force to bear in Vietnam that we can and that we should do so
 as quickly as liTe can. " But he hedged by adding" so long as this can be
 done without a \vildcat inflation and other lethal political effects ."
 He seemed to think he had found a solution in Westmoreland ' s ne,'T fasci -
 nation vTi th the sanctuaries across the borders of South Vietnam . He
 hOlled that with large numbers of troops employed in the less populated
 areas, it might be possible to have both the massive force quickly
 emIlloyed and a relatively small inflationary effect . However , he seems
 to have been misjudging I'That liTestmoreland had in mind .

 Nevertheless, his 42 billion piaster limit on U. S. military eXIJen-
 ditures vTas to become one of the controlling factors in the decision on
 Progr~m #4 strengths.

 It is true that the majority of II hard corell capt ives and defe ctors
 cited in the W~ND reports no longer predict an inevitable VC
 victory, many of this selected group nO,,7 see the war as a stale-
 mate with each side building up its res ~ect ive force . Although
 some of this group now see defeat, in the main the confidence of
 the individual enemy soldier in a military victory has dwindled
 due, i n l arge measure, to the string of defeats he has suffered
 at the hands of the US/GVN/ Fre e World Forces . However , limita -
 tion of these US/ GVN forces for economic reas ons would curtail the
 momentum of the military effort at this critical point and con-
 ceivably jeopard ize the overall US effort in Vietnam.

 4. Para 10 & 11, Part C, Ref A discusses troop utilization
 but does not depict clearly the military concept of operations in
 Vietnam for CY 67 . Our concept recognizes and is built around
 t wo equally important , continuing and compl ementary r equirernents
 which call for the same type of military re sources and flexibility
 in their application . On the one hand, ,,7e must maintain the
 security of our bases and key population and food producing centers
 and assist in expanding security of areas under Government control .
 On the other hand ,,7e must seek out and destroy the enemy ' s main
 forc es and his bases to create the environment in which meaningful
 Revolutionary Development can proceed. The priority of US/YW
 military efforts will continue to be devoted to our main mission,
 the destruction of enemy main forces and bases. The "entirely
 new kind of ,,70rk ll referred to by the Ambassador is in reality a
 continuation of our surveillance and rapid r eaction tactics vis -
 a-vis enemy forc es occupying sanctuaries in adjacent territory. We
 are according heightened emphasis to this effort , and may find it
 necessary to ask for additional forces to insure its success.

 In thi s memorandum, McNamara reveals i'Ti th striking clarity that many
 ' of the premises under Hhich the war to that point had been fought (and
 manned ) i'Tere shifting .

 He agreed with COMUSMACV that the military situation has gone
 !!somewhat better in 1966 than anticipated, !! but he found little cause
 . for optimism in the longer run . In fact, he seemed almost disheart ened
 as he noted that there was !!no reasonable Hay to bring the war to an
 end soon ." Finding an injured but undismayed opponent committed no\v to
 ''Ivai ting us out !! 'while sapping our national will and seeing "pacification
 a basic disappointment ..• no better, and if anything worse off ... " hardly
 was the kind of progress he hoped for .

 His solution vTaS to get ourselves into "a military posture tha,t ,ve
 credibly i'lould maintain indefinitely -- a posture that makes trying to
 ' wait us out' less attractive. " To do this , he proposed a five part
 program :

 (1) First, he suggested that, barring a major cha,nge in the
 war, vTe should stabilize U.S. force levels in Vietnam at about 470,000 .
 The nevI figure of 470,000 for U. S. force levels ( only 25,000 above the
 l atest figure of 445,000 for Program #3 ) apparently i'laS arrived at during
 the sessions in Saigon . Before the meetings , VJestmoreland had estj.mated
 t hat Program 3 Hou.ld entail'a piaster cost of 47 . 4 billion . The folloH-up
 papers to the conference all continued to focus upon the piaster costs of
 various troop deployments with the intent to keep them under the ~.2 billion
 Lodge cei l ing. The most probable explanation of the genesis of the 470,000
 figur e is that it r epresented the best guess at the time of the Saigon
 meeting of "That strength could be supported Ivi thin the 42 billion limit
 by making v ery strong efforts to reduce piaster costs per man .

 ( 2 ) He recommended a barrier near the DMZ and !!across the
 trails of Laos .!!

 (3) He opposed expansion of the ROLLING T1IDNDER program, r ecom-
 mending instead a !! stabilization!! to prevent the unsettling escalations
 from complicating our political situation (and negotiating posture ) and
 distracting from the main job of pacification.

 (4) He said vTe should !! pursue a vigorous pacification program"
 noting that !!progress in pacification more than anything else , will
 persuadE the enemy to negotiate or ,vithdraVT !! 51/

 ( 5) Finally, he proferred a three-sided attempt to get negoti -
 ations going by (a) shifting the pattern of our bombing (or perhaps even
 stopping it); (b) considering strategies designed to enhance the probability
 of a split beti-Teen the VC and Hanoi ; and (c) !! developing a realistic plan
 providing a role for the VC in negotiations, post"Tar life, and the govern-
 ment of the nation . "

 5. JCS Recoro~_endations
 On 4 November, the JCS forvrarded to the Secretary of Defense
 the r esults of the October PACOM Planning Conference vrith their II refine -
 ments ll added . The document, labeled JCSM 702 -66, IIDeployment of Forces
 to Meet CY Requirements, II held fe,-r surprises . The memorandum addressed
 t he crux of disagreement :

 . . . . As in past concepts, it goes beyond certain
 r estraints that have been placed on US operating forces
 to date, such as those on the air campaign in North Viet -
 nam, on cross border operations, 6n certain special opera -
 tions, and on ground actions in the southern half of the
 demilitarized zone . Further, this concept shoul d be carried
 out in its entirety, if achievement of US objectives is to
 be accomplished in the shortest time and at the least cost
 i n men and materiel. The concept describes preparation for
 operations that have not as yet been authorized , such as
 mining ports, naval quarantine, spoiling attacks and raids
 against the enemy in Cambodia and Laos, and certain special
 opera tions . Such action will support intensified and
 accelerated revolutionary development and nation building
 programs . Since the forc e requirements are based on this
 concept in its entirety, continued restraints and the
 absence of authorization for r ecommended operations could
 generate significantly different requirements for forces
 and timing. §])

 In a sense, it embraced all of the right argUI(,ents (for II intensified
 and accelerated revolutionary development and programs ll and II shortest
 t ime at the least cost, 1I an overdetermined test) but unfortunately for
 all the wrong reasons . McNamara and Johnson were not politically and
 militarily enchanted ,vith a costly me.jor force increase at that time ,
 nor 'vith cross border and air operat ions whi.ch ran grave political risks .

1~3 to 86) during the same perioJ. ~~lle i\'VA represented only 25,600 of

 Air Interdiction. The use of air pOvrer to int erdict enemy infiltration
and supply has been very great by any standard. Attack sorties in ~~os and
NVN ha;ve risen from 4750 per month at the end of last year to 9100 in 1 st
quarter of this year and to 10,600 and 12,900 in subsequent quarters. The
j.nterdiction campaign has a.bsorbed most of the increase in deployed attack-
capable aircra~t in the past years.
 A substantial air interdiction campa.ign is clearly necessary and vlorth-
while. In addition to putting a ceiling on the size of the force that can
be supported, it yields three si gnificant military effects . First, it ef-
fectively harasses and delays truck movements dOHn through the southern
panhandles of NVN and Laos, though it has no effect on troops infiltrating
on foot over trails that are virtually invisible from the air. Our ex-
per ience sho,-1s tha,t daytime armed reconnaissance above some minimum sortie
rate makes it prohibitively expensive to the enemy to attempt daylight
movement of vehicles , and so forces him to night movement . Second, des-
truction of bridges and cratering of roads forces the enemy to deploy repair
cr evls, equipmeilt, and porters to repajr or bypass the damage . Third, attacks
on vehicles, parks, and r est camps destroy some vehicles v1i th their cargo~s
and inflict ca sualties . Moreover, our bombing campaign may produce a bene-
ficial effect on U. S. and SVN morale by making NVN pay a price for its
aggression and by shovring that v1e are doing vlhat we can to interdict the
enemy. But at the scale i'le are nO"l operating, I believe our bombing is
yielding very small marginal returns, not worth the cost in pilot lives
and aj_rcraft .

 The most important problems are reflected in the belief of the rural

 \

 In view of the ARVN ' s low efficiency in maj or combat operations and
 the increasing difficulties that SVN forces have had in recruiting and
 retaining the ~lanned forces in an overtaxed economy, I believe that we
 should not increase the SVN forces (ARVN, Regional and Popular Forc es )
 above the present strength of 158 battalions with 610,000 men . It is
 likely that GVN control can be extended most rapidly by using SVN forces
 mainly for revolutionary development, and using additional recruitable
 personnel for non-military and para-military revolutionary development
 duty . The ARVN must be retrained and assigned to RD duty, and General
 Hestmoreland plans to do so . The performance of the ARVN and other SVN
 forces as an instrument for winning popular support for the GVN has been
 decidedly unsatisfactory. Apparently ARVN personnel have not appreciated
 the decisive importance of revolutionary development and popular support;
 the importance of thesei terns 1-Till be heavily emphasized in the retraining
 programs .

 Of particular interest in the Combined Campaign Plan i s the

 s,. Strategic Concept.

 VC/ J:..,lVA forces and building a viable, free nation includes

 (a ) A military offensive conducted by RVNAF
 and US/ FVlM.A.F to defeat the VC/ r:.J""VA military forces .

 (b ) Revolutionary development conducted by
 RVNAF and GVN civil elements, with the assistance of US/
 FWMA.F and US/ F'V'l civil agencies, to establish and maintain
 security in populated areas and extend legal government
 control over these areas.

 (c) Nation building conducted by GVN civil
 e l ements, vTi th the assistance of US/ F'V'l civil agencies, to
 complete the development of natiol11;'Tide political, economic ,
 and social institutions necessary for a viable , free, non-
 communist Republic of Vietnam .

 ( 2 ) The thr ee operations I'T ill take place concur -
 rently . In areas vThere there is adequate government control ,
 nation building wj.ll be in progress. In other area.s, RD will

 si ve vTill be prosecuted . Previously, the mili tary offensive

 F. What Did It Mean?

 Program 4 had important historical antecedents vrhich provide the
 basic texture of the decision-making on Program 5. The preceding
 sections have outlined the major themes and historical developments
 which projected into the succeeding program with telling effect. These
 can be briefly summari zed as follOli}"s:

 (1) A precedent, albeit a seemingly fragi l e one, of essen-
 tially saying " no " to the COMUS1>tll..CV force requirements ">'ras established.
 Actually, DoD and the President "\i}"ere beginning to question the concept
 of operation for Vietnam vrhich had l ed to programs , now becoming increas -
 i ngly costly and depressingly barren of tangible results. The illusion
 of quick victory " on the cheap" had fled, and hard reality i ntervened .
 People in and out of government were beginning to seek alternatives to
 our policies in Vietnam vri th increased interest , and F-.cogram 5 was to
 increasingly reflect this basic mood surfacing in l ate 1966.

 (2) The JCS had ad.opted a strategic concept based upon ">'ridely
 expanded operations in the North, widened and intensified operations in
 t he South designed to seek out and destroy enemy forces, and committed
 to assisting the GVN in building e.n " independent, vi able, non - conununist
 societi' -- a vestige of the unfortunate wording of NSAM 288 . §]J The
 military heads had been denied the troops they said they required to
 successfully accomplish the objectives developed under the concept, but
 t he concept itself had survived. This strategic thought was to provide
 t he conceptual baseline for F-.cogram 5.

 (3) The basic troop requirement numbers , so i mportant to
 Program 5, ">,Tere introduced during Program 4. In fact, the refined
 figure the JCS proposed in JCSM 702-66 for mid -1968, 52 4,288, became
 t he eventue.l "approved " figure for Program 5. This number remained a
 focal point throughout the planning period despite frequent impor tant
 changes in the strategic situation .

 (4) Certain " oblique alternatives, " those vrhich wer e not
 directly substituta;ble options appeared during this time -- all of them
 designed to relieve pres sure on U. S. resources, especially manpo\·rer.
 Among these weI'e the barrier plan (propos ed by McNamaI'a ), ne"\"T free
 world military force sharing formulas (KANZUS), efforts to subtly hold
 the RVNAF ' s " feet to the fire ," and operations of various kinds i n the
 " sanctuaries ."

 (5) The Reserve mobilization line - - a pol itical sound ba.:crier
 as it \'rere, remained unbroken.
 The JCS had made a two-pronged case for bI'eaking it: One,
 the.t \'l e could not adequately meet CINCPAC I s 1967 requir ements and simul -
 taneously fulfill our commitments to j\1A.TO and other threatened areas
 ">'rithout mobili zation (a,nd even then probably de.ngerously late ); and

 secondly, only such massive infusions of firepOl'rer in the North and man -
 p o"Ter in the South as they proposed could possibly achieve our war

 tions -- it appeared that the l evel was periodically studied , possibly
 negotiated, but all-,rays there -- the "Plimsoll line ." !?!U
 (6) Public disenchantment "Tith the ,'Tar ¥Tas grolving , and this
 was being manifested in diverse I.rays . On the It hawk" side povlerful
 political figures (and many l esser ones) "Tere increasingly vocal j.n
 their opposition to bombing restraints and restrictive force l evel s .
 Senat or Stennis 'I<Tas in the vanguard of this group . On the other side,

 The press Ivas moving beyond the bounds of its traditional

 the war . Acute even early on in the 'Ivar, the press opposition intensified

 (7) Failure is in the truest sense an orphan and as the sense
 of futi lity and self- doubt about achieving our objectives in Vietnam

 M.A.CV strategy narrovTed as mOre alterna.ti ves to it 'I'Tere seriously examined
